52
]
, Calabi conjectures that on a compact Käler manifold, each Käler class contains a unique Kähler metric whose Ricci form equals any prescribed real
(1,1)-
form representing the first Chern class; in
[
189
]
, Yau was able to prove Calabi conjecture and in particular that the vanishing of the first Chern class implies the existence of a Ricci-flat Kähler metric, which makes the nowhere-vanishing holomorphic volume form parallel and hence enforces the holonomy group to be a subgroup of
SU(n)
.

This stringy motivation was the first reason for complex Käler manifold with vanishing first Chern class, called
Calabi-Yau manifolds
, to receive the attention of physicists and to be largely studied along the last decades. As we will see in chapter
3
, recent developments have shown that they also play a crucial role in scattering amplitudes computation.

The vanishing of the first Chern class of a
n-
dimensional compact Kähler manifold (Calabi-Yau
n-
fold) can be proven to be equivalent to the uniqueness of a non-vanishing holomorphic
n-
form
\Omega
, that is
h^{n,0}=1
.

Morevoer, Hodge numbers turn to be constrained by the relation
h^{p,0}=h^{0,n-p}
and, if the homolomy group is exactly
SU(n)
, one has

h^{p,0}=0.

(2.114)

Therofe, the general form of the Hodge diamond for a Calabi-Yau
n-
fold with
SU(n)
holonomy group is

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}&&&&1&&&&\\[2.0pt]
&&&0&&0&&&\\[2.0pt]
&&0&&h^{1,1}&&0&&\\[2.0pt]
&\iddots&&&\vdots&&&\ddots&\\[2.0pt]
1&&h^{n-1,1}&\cdots&&\cdots&h^{1,n-1}&&1\\[2.0pt]
&\ddots&&&\vdots&&&\iddots&\\[2.0pt]
&&0&&h^{n-1,n-1}&&0&&\\[2.0pt]
&&&0&&0&&&\\[2.0pt]
&&&&1&&&&\\[2.0pt]
\end{array}

(2.115)

A simple and widely used class of CY manifolds arises as smooth projective hypersurfaces or complete intersections in complex projective space. Because the propriety to be Kähler is automatically induced by projectivity, the Calabi-Yau condition reduces to the vanishing of the first Chern class.

The complete intersection of
r
homogeneous polynomials of degrees
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
has first Chern class (see
[
83
]
)

c_{1}(X)=\left(n+1-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}\right)H_{X},

(2.116)

with
H_{X}
the hyperplane class.

The vanishing first Chern class is then subjected to the relation

n+1-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{i}=0.

(2.117)

For a smooth hypersurface
X_{d}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
of degree
d
, (
2.117
) reduces to
d=n+1
, thus one has that:

The projective algebraic variety associate to a polynomial of degree
n+1
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
is a Calabi-Yau
(n-1)
-fold.

The simplest example of a hypersurface satisfying this condition is a cubic in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, that, rather then
1-
fold Calabi-Yau, is better known as
Elliptic curve

2.2.3
Periods

Consider the Legendre family of elliptic curves

y^{2}=\mathcal{B}(x,\lambda)=x(x-1)(x-\lambda)

(2.118)

and the standard elliptic basis of the De Rham cohomology

\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\}\equiv\left\{\frac{dx}{y},\frac{x\,dx}{y}\right\}.

(2.119)

For any locally constant
1-
cycle
\gamma(\lambda)
on the fiber, we call
period
the function of
\lambda
:

\Pi(\lambda)=\int_{\gamma(\lambda)}\omega_{1}(\lambda).

(2.120)

Differentiating with respect to
\lambda
we get

\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{B}^{-3/2}(\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{B})dx=\frac{1}{2}\frac{x(x-1)}{y^{3}}dx,

(2.121)

and after noticing that the numerator can be written as

x(x-1)=\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)+\frac{2\lambda-1}{3}x-\frac{\lambda}{3},

(2.122)

we can rewrite (
2.121
) as

\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{1}=\left[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)\frac{dx}{6y^{3}}\right]+\frac{1}{6}\frac{(2\lambda-1)x-\lambda}{\mathcal{B}(x,\lambda)}\omega_{1}.

(2.123)

The term in square brackets is an exact differential
6
6

6

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\lambda)\frac{dx}{6y^{3}}=-\frac{1}{3}d\left(\frac{1}{y}\right)

thus, in cohomology, we can drop it from (
2.123
), obtaining that
\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{1}
is related to
\omega_{1}
by a rational coefficient function:

\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{1}=\frac{1}{6}\frac{(2\lambda-1)x-\lambda}{\mathcal{B}(x,\lambda)}\omega_{1}.

(2.124)

Because
\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{1}
belongs to vector space spanned by (
2.119
), there exist rational functions
a(\lambda)
and
b(\lambda)
, independent on
x
, such that

\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{1}=a(\lambda)\omega_{1}+b(\lambda)\omega_{2},

(2.125)

Comparing (
2.121
) and (
2.125
), we get

\frac{1}{2}x(x-1)=(a+bx)\mathcal{B}+\delta,

(2.126)

with
\delta
a closed
1-
form.
Assuming the ansatz
\delta=d(P/y)=(\mathcal{B}P^{\prime}-1/2P\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
, with
P(x)
a three degree polynomial, and solving term by term for the unknown coefficient, one finds:

a(\lambda)=-\frac{1}{2(\lambda-1)}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad b(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\lambda(\lambda-1)}.

(2.127)

Applying the same reduction procedure to express
\partial_{\lambda}\omega_{2}
in the above basis we obtain that the
\lambda
derivation acts on the vector basis
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})^{T}
by a
2\times 2
matrix with rational entries:

\partial_{\lambda}\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}\\
\omega_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2(\lambda-1)}&\frac{1}{2\lambda(\lambda-1)}\\
-\frac{1}{6(\lambda-1)}&\frac{2-\lambda}{6\lambda(\lambda-1)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}\\
\omega_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(2.128)

Commuting with integration in (
2.121
), we can rewrite this as a linear differential equation for the period vector
\Pi_{i}\equiv\int_{\gamma(\lambda)}\omega_{i}
as

\partial_{\lambda}\Pi_{i}=M_{ij}\Pi_{j}.

(2.129)

Solving for one of the periods, say
\Pi_{2}
, and substituting back, we get a second order differential equation for the other period, called
Picard-Fuchs equation
:

\lambda(1-\lambda)\Pi^{\prime\prime}+(1-2\lambda)\Pi^{\prime}-\frac{1}{4}\Pi=0.

(2.130)

This is not an accident occurring for elliptic curves, but a consequence of the structure of the cohomological bundle.

Let
f:X\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
be a one parameter (
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}
) family of smooth
n-
dimensional complex manifolds
X_{\lambda}=f^{-1}(\lambda)
.
The relative de Rham cohomology sheaf

\mathcal{H}_{dR}^{n}(f)\equiv R^{n}f_{\ast}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/\mathcal{M}}),

(2.131)

is a vector bundle on
\mathcal{M}
equipped with a flat connection

\nabla_{GM}:\mathcal{H}_{dR}^{n}(f)\rightarrow\Omega_{S}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{dR}^{n}(f)

(2.132)

called
Gauss-Manin connection
.

The periods of relative closed forms are the flat sections of the dual local system:

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\int_{\gamma(\lambda)}\omega(\lambda)=\int_{\gamma(\lambda)}\nabla_{GM}\omega(\lambda).

(2.133)

The steps we applied from (
2.121
) to (
2.128
) are actually part of a general precedure, called
Griffiths-Dwork reduction
, whose closure is granted by
Griffiths transversality
of the Gauss-Manin connection:

\nabla_{GM}(F^{p}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{dR}(f))\subset\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\otimes F^{p-1}\mathcal{H}^{n}_{dR}(f).

(2.134)

The Brieskorn lattice

Let
f\in\mathcal{R}\equiv\mathbb{C}[x_{0},..x_{n}]
a polynomial in
n+1
variables and
\mathcal{K}\equiv\mathcal{K}(\partial f,\mathcal{R})
the Koszul complex
[
65
,
71
]
associated to the sequence of partial derivatives

\mathcal{K}:0\rightarrow\bigwedge^{n+1}\mathcal{R}^{n+1}\xrightarrow{d_{n+1}}...\xrightarrow{d_{2}}\mathcal{R}^{n+1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}\mathcal{R}\rightarrow 0,

(2.135)

where
\mathcal{R}^{i}\equiv\otimes_{i}\mathcal{R}
and the differential acts as:

d_{i}(e_{1}\wedge...\wedge e_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{i}(-1)^{j+1}(\partial_{j}f)e_{1}\wedge...\wedge\hat{e}_{j}\wedge...\wedge e_{k}.

(2.136)

For a variety
\mathcal{V}[f]
with singular locus
\Sigma=\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i}
the homology of the associated Koszul complex satisfies

\mathcal{H}_{i}(\mathcal{K})\neq 0\quad\mbox{for}\quad i\leq dim(\Sigma)+1;

(2.137)

i.e. it is concentrated in degree zero for
\mathcal{V}[f]
smooth, and for isolated singularities the only non trivial groups are:

\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{K})=J_{f}\quad,\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathcal{K})=\frac{\{\vec{a}\in\mathcal{R}^{n+1}|\sum a_{i}\partial_{i}f=0\}}{\{\vec{b}\in\mathcal{R}^{n+1}|b_{k}=\sum_{i<j}r_{ij}(\partial_{i}fe_{j}-\partial_{j}fe_{i})\}}.

(2.138)

Remind that for any graded ring
A=\bigoplus A_{j}
, the associated Poincaré series
\mathcal{P}[A](t)
is defined to be:

\mathcal{P}[A](t)=\sum_{j\geq 0}(dimA_{j})t^{j}.

(2.139)

For isolated singularities, the “smooth” part of the Jacobian is encoded in the difference

\mathcal{P}[\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{K})](t)-\mathcal{P}[\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathcal{K})](t)=\frac{(1-t^{d-1})^{n+1}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}.

(2.140)

The form of the full Poincaré series of the Jacobian is not known in general. However, if the singular locus is a 0-dimensional complete intersection of type
(a_{1},...a_{n})
, it can be expressed in a closed rational form:

\mathcal{P}[\mathcal{H}_{0}(\mathcal{K})](t)=\frac{(1-t^{d-1})^{n+1}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}+t^{(n+1)(d-1)-\sum a_{i}}\left[\frac{\prod_{i}(1-t^{a_{i}})}{(1-t)^{n+1}}\right].

(2.141)

The global Jacobian ring
J
is not finitely generated unless the variety is smooth. However, if only isolated singularities arise, the dimensions of the graded pieces stabilize for
q>(n+1)(d-2)
to a constant dimension
\tau(\mathcal{V})
, called Tjurina number of the hypersurface. We will write:

J=dim(J_{0})\oplus dim(J_{1})\oplus...\oplus dim(J_{(n+1)(d-2)-1})\oplus\overline{\tau(\mathcal{V})},

(2.142)

using the overline for denoted all the next graded pieces have the same dimension.
The Tjurina number
\tau(\mathcal{V})
of the hypersurface is the sum of the Tjurina numbers
7
7

7

For homogenous polynomial Milnor and Tjurina numbers are equal, because of Euler relation.
of the singularities
\tau(\mathcal{V},\sigma_{j})
, corresponding, by definition, to the dimension, as a
\mathbb{C}
vector space, of the localization of the Jacobian ring at the singular locus:

\tau(\mathcal{V})=\sum\tau(\mathcal{V},\sigma_{j})=\sum dim_{\mathbb{C}}(J)_{\sigma_{i}},

(2.143)

with
(J)_{\sigma_{i}}
, for
\sigma_{i}\in\Sigma
, is

(J)_{\sigma_{i}}=\left\{\frac{f}{g}|f,g\in J,g\notin\sigma_{i}\right\}=\frac{\mathbb{C}\{x_{0},...,x_{n}\}}{(\partial_{i}f)},

(2.144)

where
\mathbb{C}\{x_{0},...,x_{n}\}
is the ring of formal power series.
There is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\frac{df\wedge\Omega^{n}}{df\wedge d\Omega^{n-1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{f}\rightarrow J_{f}\rightarrow 0,

(2.145)

where
\mathcal{B}_{f}
is called
Brieskorn lattice
.
In the case of isolated singularities the torsion is just the jacobian ring again, who is injectively mapped onto the Brieskorn lattice, thus the sequence splits

\mathcal{B}_{f}=J_{f}\oplus J_{f}.

(2.146)

2.3
Picard-Lefshetz theory

The
Picard-Lefschetz theory

[
111
]
is the complex analogous of the Morse theory that studies the topology of level sets of complex analytic functions and provides a concrete tool for computing monodromies.

Let us begin by considering the following simple example of a two variable function:

f(z,w)=z^{2}+w^{2}.

(2.147)

The function
f(z,w)
has a unique critical point:

\begin{cases}\partial_{z}f(z,w)=0\\
\partial_{w}f(z,w)=0\end{cases}\quad\quad\longrightarrow\quad\quad(z,w)=(0,0).

(2.148)

We refer to the value of the function
f(z,w)
at a critical point as a
critical value
, in the present case
f(0,0)=0
. The
critical set
is the set of points in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
where the function
f
takes the critical value:

V_{0}=\left\{(z,w)|z^{2}+w^{2}=0\right\}.

(2.149)

For any other value
f(z,w)=t
, different from the critical one, we call
level sets
the loci

V_{t}=\left\{(z,w)|z^{2}+w^{2}=t\right\}.

(2.150)

In order to figure out the topology of these level sets we can consider the Riemann surfaces associated with the function (
2.147
)

w=\sqrt{(t-z^{2})}.

(2.151)

These surfaces can be obtained gluing together two copies of the complex plane
z
with a cut along the segment
(-\sqrt{t},\sqrt{t})
, as showed in Figure
2.5
, resulting in a surface topologically equivalent to a cylinder. When
t=0
, the corresponding critical level set consists of two lines intersecting at the point
0
.

Figure 2.5:
Gluing of the two Riemann sheets along the two edges of the cut from
-\sqrt{t}
to
\sqrt{t}
. The resulting surface is topologically equivalent to a cylinder.

Consider now the fibration
f:\mathbb{C}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\ast}_{t}
over the space
\mathbb{C}_{t}^{\ast}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, which fibers are Riemann surfaces representing the non-critical level sets
V_{t}
. Note that we removed from the base the point
t=0
, which correspond to the singular fiber
V_{0}
. Let us now consider the circular path around
t=0

t(\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}\alpha\quad\quad 0\leq\tau\leq 1,\quad\alpha>0.

(2.152)

Figure 2.6:
Action of the monodromy on the vanishing and covanishing cycles.

We aim to study how the fiber
V_{t}
varies along this path by tracking the motion of the branch points
z=\pm\sqrt{t(\tau)}
in the complex planes
\mathbb{C}_{z}^{\pm}
as the parameter
\tau
evolves. We observe that these branch points rotate counterclockwise around
z=0
undergoing a half-turn (rotation by
\pi
) at
\tau=1
(see Figure
2.6
).
Considering the fibration
V\longrightarrow I
, with
I\equiv[0,1]
, defined by
V_{t(\tau)}\longrightarrow\tau
, we can associate to the closed curve
t(\tau)
in (
2.152
) a continuous map

\displaystyle\Gamma:V\longrightarrow V_{t(0)},

(2.153)

such that for any
\tau
, the map
\Gamma_{\tau}:V_{t(\tau)}\longrightarrow V_{t(0)}
defined by
\Gamma_{\tau}(\cdot)=\Gamma(\tau,\cdot)
is a diffeomorphism, and
\Gamma_{0}=id
. Since
t(1)=t(0)
, the corresponding map
h=\Gamma_{1}:V_{t(0)}\longrightarrow V_{t(1)}
is called
monodromy map
.

We are interested to know how this map acts on the first homology group of the fiber
V_{t}
, which is generated by the 1-cycle
\Delta
represented in Figure
2.5
. This
1
-cycle
\Delta
is called
Picard-Lefschetz vanishing cycle
, due to the fact it shrinks to a point when
t\rightarrow 0
. Its transversely intersecting cycle
\nabla
is called
covanishing cycle
and it generates the first homology group
H^{BM}_{1}\left(V_{t},\mathbb{Z}\right)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Here,
H^{BM}_{1}(V_{t})
is the first Borel-Moore homology of the non-compact space
V_{t}
. This homology admits chains that may be infinite in extent but are restricted to be finite in any compact region. A vertical line in the cilinder
V_{t}
is locally finite because, in any compact sub-interval of the vertical direction the line is finite. Even though the line can extend indefinitely along the cylinder, within any small, bounded region it is just a finite segment. Since
H^{BM}_{1}\left(V_{t},\mathbb{Z}\right)\simeq H_{1}\left(C,\partial C;\mathbb{Z}\right)
, where
C
is the compact cylinder, it is easy to prove that
H^{BM}_{1}\left(V_{t},\mathbb{Z}\right)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
with generator
\nabla
.

In Figure
2.6
we show how the diffeomorphism
\Gamma_{\tau}
acts on these two cycles. In the
\mathbb{C_{z}^{-}}
foil, after the action of
\Gamma_{\tau}
,
\nabla^{-}
is rigidly transported along the counterclockwise direction of the rotation. As depicted in Figure
2.7
we can deform homotopically the support of
\nabla^{-}_{\tau}
to the support of
\nabla^{-}_{0}
through a connected path
\gamma
in
\mathbb{C}_{z}^{-}
such that:

\nabla_{\tau}=\nabla^{+}_{\tau}+\nabla^{-}_{\tau}\thickapprox\nabla^{+}_{0}+\tilde{\nabla}^{-}=\nabla^{+}_{0}+\gamma+\nabla_{0}^{-}.

(2.154)

In terms of the homology we have:

\left[\nabla_{\tau}\right]\sim\left[\nabla_{0}\right]+\left[\gamma\right]\sim\left[\nabla_{0}\right],

(2.155)

if
\gamma
in contractible. If
\tau=1
the path
\gamma
closes around the hole and

\left[\nabla_{1}\right]=\nabla_{0}+\Delta.

(2.156)

Figure 2.7:
Homotopically deformation of
\nabla^{-}_{\tau}
over
\nabla^{-}_{0}
.

In particular, one gets that, up to homotopies, the monodromy map
h
acts as the identity outside a compact set around
z=0
and non-trivially inside this set. More precisely, it maps the vanishing cycle
\Delta
into itself and it acts as the (homotopically equivalent) identity in the part of
\nabla
extending outside the compact set and with the following transformation inside the set:

h\nabla=\nabla-\Delta.

(2.157)

It is worth to mention that while the map
h
defined on the fibration
V\to I
depends on the choice of the diffeomorphisms
\Gamma
, when induced to the (co)homology it becomes independent on such a choice.
This action allows us to define a function, called
variation map
, mapping a cycle with closed support to a cycle with compact support:

\begin{split}\rm{Var}\quad:\quad H_{1}^{BM}\left(V_{t},\mathbb{Z}\right)\quad&\longrightarrow\quad H_{1}\left(V_{t},\mathbb{Z}\right),\\
\nabla\quad&\longrightarrow\quad(\Delta\circ\nabla)\Delta,\\
\end{split}

(2.158)

where
(\Delta\circ\nabla)
denotes the intersection pairing between the cycles
\Delta
and
\nabla
, defined as the number of topological intersections counted with a sign depending by the relative orientation of the two cycles.

The main objects we defined so far are the
vanishing cycles
, the
monodromy
and the
variation map
for a two-variables function. Defining these objects for arbitrary functions of several variables is a challenging problem that remains unsolved in general. Picard-Lefschetz theory offers a powerful method to address this problem by using
deformation theory
techniques.

2.3.1
Monodromy, variation operators and vanishing cycles

In this paragraph, we extend the above discussion to the case of holomorphic functions in several complex variables and provide formal definitions of the concepts introduced in the previous example.

Let

f:\mathcal{M}^{n}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{C}

(2.159)

be a holomorphic function on a
n-
dimensional complex manifold
\mathcal{M}^{n}
. Let
U
be a contractible compact region in the target space with smooth boundary
\partial U
, and let us assume
f
has a finite number of critical points
\Sigma=\left\{\sigma_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\mu}
with critical values
t_{i}=f\left(\sigma_{i}\right)
on
\overline{U}
. Let us indicate with
F_{t}
the level set of the function
f
at
t\in U
:

F_{t}=\left\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}|f(\mathbf{z})=t\right\}.

(2.160)

If
t\in U
is not a critical value, then
F_{t}
is a
(n-1)-
dimensional complex manifold with smooth boundary. Let
t_{0}
be a non-critical value in the boundary
\partial U
and let us construct for each class of loops
\left[\gamma\right]\in\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},t_{0}\right)
a continuous family of mappings
\Gamma_{\tau}:F_{t(\tau)}\longrightarrow F_{t_{0}}
, for which
\Gamma_{0}=id
. Then,
h_{\gamma}\equiv\Gamma_{1}
, transforming the non-singular level
F_{t_{0}}
into itself, defines the
monodromy map
along the loop
\gamma
. Note that the map
h_{\gamma}
depends on the specific path
\gamma
we are considering.

Definition 2.14
.

(Monodromy operator)

We call monodromy operator of the loop
\gamma
the action
h_{\gamma\ast}=h_{\left[\gamma\right]}
of the transformation
h_{\gamma}
on the homology of the non-singular level set
H_{\ast}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)
.

The transformation
h_{\gamma}
also induces an automorphism
h_{\left[\gamma\right]}^{(r)}
in the relative homology group
H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)
of the non-singular level set
F_{t_{0}}
modulo its boundary. This homology is isomorphic to the homology of cycles with closed support:

H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)\simeq H^{BM}_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}}\setminus\partial F_{t_{0}}\right).

(2.161)

Since the action
h_{\gamma}
is trivial on the boundary
\partial F_{t}
, then the difference between
h_{\left[\gamma\right]}^{(r)}\delta
and
\delta\in H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)
is a cycle in
H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)
.

Definition 2.15
.

(Variation operator)

The homomorphism

\rm{Var}_{\gamma}\quad:\quad H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)\quad\longrightarrow\quad H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)\\

(2.162)

is called the variation operator over the loop
\gamma
.

Using the natural homomorphism

i_{\ast}\quad:\quad H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)\quad\longrightarrow\quad H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)

(2.163)

induced by the inclusion
F_{t_{0}}\subset\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)
, we can write the following relations connecting the automorphisms
h_{\left[\gamma\right]}
and
h_{\left[\gamma\right]}^{(r)}
:

\begin{split}&h_{\left[\gamma\right]}=id+\rm{Var}_{\gamma}\cdot i_{\ast}\,,\\
&h_{\left[\gamma\right]}^{(r)}=id+i_{\ast}\cdot\rm{Var}_{\gamma}\,.\end{split}

(2.164)

If the class
\left[\gamma\right]\in\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},z_{0}\right)
is given by
\left[\gamma\right]=\left[\gamma_{1}\right]\cdot\left[\gamma_{2}\right]
,
8
8

8

Note that in the composition of homology classes, we follow the convention of right multiplication.
then

\begin{split}&\rm{Var}_{\gamma}=\rm{Var}_{\gamma_{1}}+\rm{Var}_{\gamma_{2}}+\rm{Var}_{\gamma_{2}}\cdot i_{\ast}\cdot\rm{Var}_{\gamma_{1}},\\
&h_{\left[\gamma\right]}=h_{\left[\gamma_{2}\right]}\cdot h_{\left[\gamma_{1}\right]},\\
&h_{\left[\gamma\right]}^{(r)}=h_{\left[\gamma_{2}\right]}^{(r)}\cdot h_{\left[\gamma_{1}\right]}^{(r)}.\\
\end{split}

(2.165)

Let us suppose all the critical points are non-degenerate, i.e. the Hessian of
f
at any critical point is non-degenerate, and suppose the corresponding critical values are different:
9
9

9

This second requirement is not strictly necessary to define a Morse function.
such a function is said to be
Morse
.

Definition 2.16
.

(Monodromy group)

The map

\begin{split}\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},z_{0}\right)\quad&\longrightarrow\quad Aut\left(H_{\bullet}(F_{t_{0}})\right),\\
[\gamma]\quad&\longrightarrow h_{\gamma\ast}\\
\\
\end{split}

(2.166)

is called monodromy representation of
\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},t_{0}\right)
. The imagine of this map defines what we call the Monodromy group of the Morse function
f
.

Now, we construct a path
u:\left[0,1\right]\longrightarrow U
joining the non-critical value
t_{0}=u(0)\in\partial U
to some critical value
t_{i}=u(1)\in U
without crossing any other critical value. The Morse lemma tells us that, given a holomorphic Morse function
f
, it always exists a local set of coordinates in a neighbourhood of the non-degenerate critical point
p_{i}
such that the function takes the form

f(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=t_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}^{2}.

(2.167)

Then, for each path
u
, we can define a family of
(n-1)-
dimensional spheres in the level manifolds
F_{u(\tau)}
. For each point of the path
u(\tau)
the level set
F_{u(\tau)}
is a hyperboloid equivalent to a trivial fibration with base a
(n-1)-
dimensional sphere of radius
\sqrt{|u(\tau)-t_{i}|}
:

S(\tau)=\sqrt{u(\tau)-t_{i}}S^{n-1}.

(2.168)

In particular, we have that the sphere
S(1)
reduces to the critical point
p_{i}
.

Definition 2.17
.

(Picard-Lefschetz vanishing cycle)

The homology class
\Delta\in H_{n-1}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)
represented by the
(n-1)-
dimensional sphere
S(0)
in
F_{t_{0}}
is called vanishing cycle of Picard-Lefschetz along the path
u
.

Note that the homotopy class of
u\in U
uniquely defines the homology class of the vanishing cycle
\Delta
modulo orientation.

Definition 2.18
.

(Distinguished Basis)

The set of cycles
\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{\mu}\in H_{n-1}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)
, with
t_{0}
non-singular, is called distinguished if:

(i)

The cycles
\Delta_{i}
are vanishing along non-self-intersecting paths
u_{i}
reaching the critical values
t_{i}
;

(ii)

The unique common point of
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
for
i\neq j
is
u_{i}(0)=u_{j}(0)=t_{0}
;

(iii)

The paths
u_{i},\dots,u_{\mu}
are numbered in the order in which they enter to the point
t_{0}
counting clockwise starting from the boundary
\partial U
of
U
.

Example 2.3.1
.

f(z)=z^{3}-3\lambda z
.

Let us consider the Morse function
f(x)=z^{3}-3\lambda z
, with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and let us construct a distinguished basis of vanishing cycles. This function is a deformation of the function
f(z)=z^{3}
and it has two critical points in the real line

\mathcal{C}:\quad\bar{z}_{1}=\sqrt{\lambda},\quad\quad\bar{z}_{2}=-\sqrt{\lambda},

(2.169)

with corresponding critical values are

t_{1}=-2\lambda\sqrt{\lambda},\quad\quad t_{2}=2\lambda\sqrt{\lambda}.

(2.170)

Let us choose as non-critical reference point
t_{0}=0
and let us construct the paths
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
connecting the critical values with
t_{0}
. The level manifold at
t_{0}=0
consists of three points:

F_{t_{0}}:\quad z^{3}-3\lambda z=0\quad\rightarrow z_{1}=-\sqrt{3\lambda},\quad z_{2}=0,\quad z_{3}=\sqrt{3\lambda}.

(2.171)

In this example the level manifold for a generic regular point
t
is given by the condition
f(z)=t
which admits three point solutions. The vanishing cycles, when we approach the critical values
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, are the differences

\Delta_{1}=\left\{z_{3}\right\}-\left\{z_{2}\right\},\quad\quad\quad\Delta_{2}=\left\{z_{2}\right\}-\left\{z_{1}\right\}

(2.172)

between the zeroth homology classes represented by the points.

Choosing the set
U
as depicted in Figure
2.8
the cycles
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
form a distingushed basis for
H_{1}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)
.

Figure 2.8
:
Choice of the set U and the paths
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
in the codomain of the function
f(z)=z^{3}-3\lambda z
.

Example 2.3.2
.

f(x,y)=x^{3}-3\lambda x+y^{2}
.

In this second example we consider the function of two variables
f(x,y)=x^{3}-3\lambda x+y^{2}
, which is a deformation through the small real parameter
\lambda
of the function
f(x,y)=x^{3}+y^{2}
. The set of critical points in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with their corresponding critical values is

\mathcal{C}:\quad\begin{cases}\,P_{1}\,:\,\,\left(x,y\right)=\left(\sqrt{\lambda},0\right)\quad&\rightarrow\quad t_{1}=-2\lambda\sqrt{\lambda}\\
\,P_{2}\,:\,\,\left(x,y\right)=\left(-\sqrt{\lambda},0\right)\quad&\rightarrow\quad t_{2}=2\lambda\sqrt{\lambda}.\\
\end{cases}

(2.173)

As in the previous example we can consider the paths
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
joining the two critical values with the non-critical value
t_{0}=0
.
The level manifold in this regular point is the graph of the two-valued function
y=\pm\sqrt{x^{3}-3\lambda x}
, namely the double-covering of the
x
complex plane branched between the points
x_{1}=-\sqrt{3\lambda}
and
x_{2}=0
and
x_{3}=\sqrt{3\lambda}
and infinity.

As we move the value of
t
from
0
to one of the two critical values, the level manifold
f(x,y)=t
is deformed and becomes singular at
t=t_{1}
and
t=t_{2}
. In particular, when we approach
t_{1}
we have that the branch point
x_{2}
moves until it overlaps
x_{3}
, while, when we approach
t_{2}
the point
x_{2}
moves towards the point
x_{1}
. From this construction, we can draw the vanishing cycles corresponding to the paths
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
: we obtain
\Delta_{1}
encircling the points
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
, and
\Delta_{2}
encircling the points
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
(see Figure
2.9
).

Figure 2.9
:
Vanishing and Covanishing cycles.

Definition 2.19
.

(Simple loops)

A simple loop is an element
\tau_{i}
of
\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},t_{0}\right)
represented by the loop going along the path
u_{i}
from
t_{0}
to
t_{i}
, then encircling
t_{i}
with a anticlockwise path and returning along
u_{i}
to
t_{0}
.

The region
\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},t_{0}\right)
is homotopically equivalent to a bouquet of
\mu
circles. Then, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},t_{0}\right)
is a free group with
\mu
generators
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{\mu}
.

Definition 2.20
.

(Weakly distinguished)

The set of vanishing cycles
\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{\mu}
defined by the paths
u_{1},\dots,u_{\mu}
is called weakly distinguished if
\pi_{1}\left(U\setminus\left\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\mu}\right\},t_{0}\right)
is the free group generated by the simple loops
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{\mu}
associated to the paths
u_{1},\dots,u_{\mu}
.

We have that if the paths
\left\{u_{i}|i=1,\dots,\mu\right\}
define a weakly distinguished set of vanishing cycles
\Delta_{i}
in the
(n-1)-
homology group of the non-singular level manifold, then, the monodromy group of the function
f
is generated by the monodromy operators
h_{\tau_{i}\ast}=h_{\left[\tau_{i}\right]}
. Hence, the monodromy group of
f
is always a group generated by
\mu
generators.

Definition 2.21
.

(Picard-Lefschetz operator)

The monodromy operator

h_{i}=h_{\tau_{i}\ast}\,:\,\,H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)\,\longrightarrow\,H_{\bullet}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)

(2.174)

of the simple loop
\tau_{i}
is called the
i^{\rm th}
Picard-Lefschetz operator.

In the example
2.3.1
, we can take trace of the change of the position of the three points
z_{i}
when we move
t
along the paths
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
. We observe that along the path
\tau_{1}
the point
z_{2}
approaches the point
z_{3}
, then they make a half-turn around a common centre and move again away one from the other. The point
z_{1}
stays fixed. Then, we deduce the following monodromy action on the vanishing cycles:

h_{1}\Delta_{1}=-\Delta_{1},\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad h_{1}\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}.

(2.175)

In the same way we can deduce

h_{2}\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2},\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad h_{2}\Delta_{2}=-\Delta_{2}.

(2.176)

The cycles
\Delta_{i}
are in the homology group
H_{n-1}\left(F_{t}\right)
of the non singular level manifold
F_{t}
. Moreover, we are interested also in the homology group
H_{n-1}\left(F_{t},\partial F_{t}\right)
, which is dual to the group
H_{n-1}\left(F_{t}\right)
. In the present case it is generated by two cycles
\nabla_{i}
such that

\left(\nabla_{i}\circ\Delta_{j}\right)=\delta_{ij}.

(2.177)

We can choose

\nabla_{1}=\left\{z_{3}\right\}\quad,\quad\nabla_{2}=-\left\{z_{1}\right\},

(2.178)

for which we have the following variations:

\begin{split}&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{1}}\nabla_{1}=\left\{z_{2}\right\}-\left\{z_{3}\right\}=-\Delta_{1},\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{1}}\nabla_{2}=0,\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{2}}\nabla_{1}=0,\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{2}}\nabla_{2}=-\left\{z_{2}\right\}+\left\{z_{1}\right\}=-\Delta_{2}.\\
\end{split}

(2.179)

We can now consider the loop
\tau=\tau_{2}\tau_{1}
that turns around the point
t_{0}=0
encircling the two critical values
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
in a positive counterclockwise direction. The monodromy transformation associated to this loop permutes the points
z_{3}\longrightarrow z_{2}\longrightarrow z_{1}\longrightarrow z_{3}
, then,

\begin{split}&h_{\tau}\Delta_{1}=\left\{z_{2}\right\}-\left\{z_{1}\right\}=\Delta_{2},\\
&h_{\tau}\Delta_{2}=\left\{z_{1}\right\}-\left\{z_{3}\right\}=-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2},\\
\end{split}

(2.180)

and

\begin{split}&\rm{Var}_{\tau}\nabla_{1}=\left\{z_{2}\right\}-\left\{z_{3}\right\}=-\Delta_{1},\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau}\nabla_{2}=-\left\{z_{3}\right\}+\left\{z_{1}\right\}=-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}.\end{split}

(2.181)

The monodromy group of the Morse function
f
is generated by the Picard-Lefschetz operators
h_{\tau_{1}}
and
h_{\tau_{2}}
. All the elements of this group preserve the intersection product of the group
H_{0}\left(F_{t}\right)
, for
t
non-critical, generated by the vanishing cycles
\Delta_{i}
. The monodromy group is the group
S_{3}
of permutations of three elements.

Now, let us construct the monodromy group for the example
2.3.2
. Drawing the analogous of the Figure
2.7
, we can deduce the action of the Picard-Lefschetz operator on the vanishing cycles to be

\begin{split}&h_{1}\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{1},\\
&h_{1}\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2},\\
&h_{2}\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2},\\
&h_{2}\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{2},\end{split}

(2.182)

and the following variation on the dual cycles:

\begin{split}&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{1}}\nabla_{1}=-\Delta_{1},\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{1}}\nabla_{2}=0,\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{2}}\nabla_{1}=0,\\
&\rm{Var}_{\tau_{2}}\nabla_{2}=-\Delta_{2}.\end{split}

(2.183)

The monodromy group of the Morse function
f(x,y)
is isomorphic to the group of non-singular
2\times 2
integer matrices with determinant
1
. The group is generated by the action of the Picard-Lefschetz operators on the vanishing cycles, given by

M_{1}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{matrix}\right)\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad M_{2}=\left(\begin{matrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right).

(2.184)

These methods, explicitly shown in one or two complex dimensions, can in principle be extended to higher dimensions to determine vanishing cycles, their duals, and the action of the monodromy group on them.

2.3.2
Picard-Lefschetz Theorem

The Picard-Lefschetz theorem establishes a relation between the variation of (co)-vanishing cycles due to the action of the monodromy operator with their intersection product in
H_{n-1}\times H_{n-1}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.

Let us start considering the simple loop
\tau_{i}
associated with the path
u_{i}
connecting the non-critical reference point
t_{0}\in\mathbb{C}_{t}
to the critical value
t_{i}\in\mathbb{C}_{t}
. Let us assume that the critical value is
t_{i}=0
, so that in some local coordinates around the critical point
P_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we can write the function
f
in the form

f(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}^{2}.

(2.185)

If we intersect
f^{-1}(t_{0})
with the ball
\sum_{j}|z_{j}|^{2}\leq 4\epsilon^{2}
, the non-critical value
t_{0}
is sufficiently close to the critical value
0
, say
|t_{0}|=\epsilon^{2}
. We can suppose that all other critical values of
f
are outside the disk of radius
4\epsilon^{2}
in
\mathbb{C}_{t}
, so that our simple loop encircles just one singularity.

Let us define the ball
\overline{B}_{2\epsilon}
of radius
2\epsilon
in the space
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\overline{B}_{2\epsilon}=\left\{\left(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\right)|r\leq 2\epsilon\right\},

(2.186)

and let us call
\tilde{F}_{t}
the intersection of the level set
F_{t}
with this ball.

Lemma 2.2
.

(Transversality)

For
|t|<4\epsilon^{2}
, the level set
F_{t}
is transverse to the
(2n-1)
-dimensional sphere
\partial\overline{B}_{2\epsilon}
.

From this lemma it follows that for
0<|t|<4\epsilon^{2}
the sets
\tilde{F}_{t}=F_{t}\cap\overline{B}_{2\epsilon}
are diffeomorphic manifolds with boundary, while
\tilde{F}_{0}
is a cone with vertex in zero.

Lemma 2.3
.

(Bouquet of Spheres)

For
0<|t|<4\epsilon^{2}
, the manifold
\tilde{F}_{t}
is diffeomorphic to the disk sub-bundle of the tangent bundle of the standard
(n-1)
dimensional sphere
S^{n-1}
.

From this second lemma follows the following result:

Lemma 2.4
.

(Self-intersection)

The self-intersection number of vanishing cycle
\Delta
in the complex manifold
\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}
is equal to

\left(\Delta\circ\Delta\right)=(-1)^{(n-1)(n-2)/2}\left(1+(-1)^{n-1}\right)\,=\,\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{for}\quad n=0\,\mathrm{mod}\,2,\\
+2\quad&\text{for}\quad n=1\,\mathrm{mod}\,4,\\
-2\quad&\text{for}\quad n=3\,\mathrm{mod}\,4.\\
\end{cases}

(2.187)

Poincaré duality
2.88
for a compact manifold
X
of dimension
n
states that
H^{k}(X)\sim H_{n-k}(X)
. If
X
is noncompact, while for cohomology it is not a problem, for homology one has to introduce Borel-Moore homology for which one has
H^{k}(X)\sim H^{BM}_{n-k}(X)
, see
[
92
]
. Hence, in our case, we get
H_{k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)\sim H^{BM}_{n-k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
, and
H_{k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)\sim H^{k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)\sim H^{k}(S^{n-1})
.
Therefore, the relative homology group
H_{k}(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}})
is zero for
k\neq n-1
, while
H_{n-1}(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}})
is isomorphic to the
\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover the latter is generated by the relative cycle
\nabla
dual to
\Delta
such that
\Delta\circ\nabla=1
.

In general, a relative cycle
\delta\in H_{k}\left(F_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial F_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
can be represented in the form

\delta=\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}

(2.188)

where
\delta_{1}\in H_{k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
and
\delta_{2}
is a chain in
F_{\epsilon^{2}}\setminus B_{2\epsilon}
. The transformation
h_{\tau}=\Gamma_{1}
is the identity in
F_{\epsilon^{2}}\setminus B_{2\epsilon}
, hence, it acts non-trivially only on the cycle
\delta_{1}
. Therfore,
\rm{Var}_{\tau}(\delta)=\rm{Var}_{\tau}(\delta_{1})
.

Since
H_{k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)=\langle\nabla\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, then
\delta_{1}=m\cdot\nabla
, with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m=\delta\circ\Delta
, and, in order to compute the action of the variation operator on
H_{k}\left(\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}},\partial\tilde{F}_{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
, it is sufficient to calculate its action on
\nabla
.

Theorem 2.13
.

(Picard-Lefschetz)

Under the above hypotheses

\rm{Var}_{\tau}\left(\nabla\right)=(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}\Delta.

(2.189)

It follows that for
a\in H\left(F_{t_{0}},\partial F_{t_{0}}\right)
:

\displaystyle\rm{Var}_{\tau}(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}\left(a\circ\Delta\right)\Delta,

(2.190)

\displaystyle h^{(r)}_{\tau}(a)

\displaystyle=a+(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}\left(a\circ\Delta\right)i_{\ast}\Delta;

(2.191)

and for
a\in H_{n-1}\left(F_{t_{0}}\right)

h_{\tau}(a)=a+(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}\left(a\circ\Delta\right)\Delta,

(2.192)

where
i_{\ast}
is the homomorphism (
2.163
).

Example 2.3.3
.

In this example we compute the monodromy matrices and intersection numbers, needed in section
4.4.1
for the analysis of the Pearcey integral.

Let
\Delta_{1}=\{z_{3}\}-\{z_{4}\}
be the vanishing cycle associated to the singular point
\sigma_{1}
, meaning the two points
\{z_{3}\}
and
\{z_{4}\}
coalesce when approaching to
\sigma_{1}
. The self intersection number

\Delta_{1}\circ\Delta_{1}=2,

(2.193)

can be easily computed by setting
n=1
in equation (
2.187
).

For the sake of clarity, being careful not to make confusion among vanishing and co-vanishing cycles, is useful here to re-wright (
2.192
) with adapted notation:

h_{j}(\Delta_{i})=\Delta_{i}+(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}\left(\Delta_{i}\circ\Delta_{j}\right)\Delta_{j},

(2.194)

We can now apply (
2.194
), for evaluating the monodromy action on
\Delta_{1}
when going around the singular point
\sigma_{1}
:

h_{1}(\Delta_{1})=\Delta_{1}-(\Delta_{1}\circ\Delta_{1})\Delta_{1}=-\Delta_{1}.

(2.195)

The transformed vanishing cycle

\Delta_{1}^{(1)}\equiv h_{1}(\Delta_{1})=\{z_{4}\}-\{z_{3}\}.

(2.196)

reveals the flipping
\{z_{3}\}\leftrightarrow\{z_{4}\}
. We can now use this information to determine the action on the co-vanishing cycle
\Delta_{2}=\{z_{1}\}-\{z_{4}\}
:

\Delta_{2}^{(1)}=\Delta_{2}(\{z_{3}\}\leftrightarrow\{z_{4}\})=\{z_{1}\}-\{z_{3}\}.

(2.197)

In order to express
\Delta_{2}^{(1)}
in terms of
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, we use the trick to insert
\{z_{4}\}-\{z_{4}\}=0
in the previous equation, getting:

\Delta_{2}^{(1)}=\{z_{1}\}+(\{z_{4}\}-\{z_{4}\})-\{z_{3}\}=(\{z_{1}\}-(\{z_{4}\})+(\{z_{4}\})-\{z_{3}\})=\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}.

(2.198)

Proceeding analogously for
\Delta_{3}
, we get

\Delta_{3}^{(1)}=\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}.

(2.199)

Packaging relations (
2.195
),(
2.198
) and (
2.199
) in the representation of the monodromy, around
\sigma_{1}
, acting on the basis of vanishing cycles, i.e.

\Delta_{i}^{(1)}=(M_{1})_{ij}\Delta_{j},

(2.200)

we get the first of the matrices reported in (
4.108
):

M_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
-1&1&0\\
-1&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(2.201)

In order to obtain
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
we simply repeat the very same procedure considering the singular points
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
cases, respectively.

Finally, we can use monodromy matrices to extract the intersection numbers among vanishing cycle. Inverting (
2.194
), we get

\Delta_{i}\circ\Delta_{j}=[\Delta_{i}-h_{j}(\Delta_{i})]\Delta_{j}^{-1}.

(2.202)

By contruction, we have
h_{j}(\Delta_{i})={(M_{j})}_{ik}\Delta_{k}
, so when can rewrite Picard-Lefschetz formula with an explicit dependence on the monodromy matrices as

\Delta_{i}\circ\Delta_{j}=[\Delta_{i}-{(M_{j})}_{ik}\Delta_{k}]\Delta_{j}^{-1}.

(2.203)

Let us apply (
2.203
) to compute, for instance, the intersections

\begin{split}\Delta_{1}\circ\Delta_{2}&=[\Delta_{1}-(M_{2})_{1k}\Delta_{k}]\Delta_{2}^{-1}\\
&=[\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}]\Delta_{2}^{-1}=1,\end{split}

(2.204)

and

\begin{split}\Delta_{2}\circ\Delta_{1}&=[\Delta_{2}-(M_{1})_{2k}\Delta_{k}]\Delta_{1}^{-1}\\
&=[\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}]\Delta_{1}^{-1}=1,\end{split}

(2.205)

revealing the intersection product is symmetric, as expected for even
n-1
.

3

Perturbative theory via Intersection theory

Given a classical model described by fields
\bm{\phi}
with action
S[\bm{\phi}]
, the behavior of the corresponding quantum model is encoded in the partition function

Z=\int\mathcal{D}\bm{\phi}\,e^{iS[\bm{\phi}]}.

(3.1)

Because of Haag’s theorem, the only well-defined physical states of a quantum theory are the free (non-interacting) states. The best we can do, for a interacting theory, is thus to define the states in the asympotic regions where the coupling tends to vanish and to study the interaction by observing the evolution of a state from an asymptotic region to another, that is via a scattering process. In practice, what one does is to fix the initial (incoming) and final (outgoing) states, and to compute the probability that such evolution occurs, encoded in the
Scattering matrix
. For a scattering process involving
n
states, the scattering matrix can be shown to be related to the so called
n-
points Green function:

G^{(n)}\equiv Z^{-1}\int\mathcal{D}\bm{\phi}\,\phi_{1}\dots\phi_{n}\,e^{iS[\bm{\phi}]}.

(3.2)

If we knew a general closed-form solution for path integral computation, this chapter could end here. Unfortunately, we don’t; even worse, we can compute it only in one case: when
S
is at most quadratic in
\phi
and the path integral is gaussian. Except for the free theory, only in some very special case, with extra structure, this is enough to fully solve the theory; examples include certain topological field theories (e.g. Chern-Simons), integral QFTs and statitistical models in low dimension (e.g. sine-Gordon, Liouville), theories amenable to supersymmetric localization (e.g.
N=2
SUSY on
S^{4}
). In generic quantum field theories, statistical systems and string theory, exact path-integral evaluation is not available; one must therefore resort to non-perturbative techniques, as instanton calculus, Lattice field theory and mean-field methods, or approximation techniques, expanding either around the classical solution (Saddle-point) in powers of
\hbar
, or around the free theory in powers of the coupling constant: this is
perturbative theory
.

In order to perturbatively expand the
n-
points Green’s functions, considering only one scalar field for the sake of simplicity, it is convenient to slightly modify the partition function (
3.1
) by adding an external auxiliary source
J
, defining the partition functional
1
1

1

By
\langle-\rangle
we denote space-time integration.

Z[J]=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,e^{i\left(S[\phi]+\langle J\phi\rangle\right)},

(3.3)

with respect to which, the
n-
points Green’s functions can be written as

G^{(n)}=(-i)^{n}\frac{\delta^{n}}{\delta J_{1}\dots\delta J_{n}}Z[J]\Big|_{J=0}.

(3.4)

Let us now split the action as
S=S_{0}+S_{I}
, with
S_{0}
the free quadratic part and
S_{I}
the interacting one. Denoting by
Z_{0}[J]
the partition functional of the free theory, it is quite easy to show that one can rewrite the full partition functional as

\begin{split}Z[J]&=e^{iS_{I}\left[-i\frac{\delta}{\delta J}\right]}Z_{0}[J]\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}(i)^{k}\left(S_{I}\left[-i\frac{\delta}{\delta J}\right]\right)^{k}Z_{0}[J],\end{split}

(3.5)

obtaining an expansion in terms of a gaussian partition function, whose standard solution, encoding Wick’s theorem, is written in terms of the inverse of the quadratic operator, called
Feynman propagator

\Delta_{F}
, as the sum over all possible pairings
P
(contraction):

G^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})_{0}=\sum_{P}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in P}\Delta_{F}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

(3.6)

Remarkably, all order of the expansion can be systematically written by the two-point propagator, the interaction vertex and suitable prescriptions (
Feynman rules
); this leads to
Feynman diagrams
.

The story is a little more involving in the case of string theory, because interaction cannot be switch off and a free theory cannot be isolated: the interaction coupling constant is an internal variable arising from dynamic, precisely the vacuum expectation value of a particular string state, the dilaton; as a consequence string theory is intrinsically interacting and free string theory is meaningless. Moreover, conformal invariance constrains strings to be on-shell, then off-shell amplitudes are not well-defined.
2
2

2

See
[
171
]
for a review
.

However, the advantage of conformal invariance allows to perturbatively treat the partition function associated to the Polyakov action and to express the bosonic
n-
string amplitude as a topological expansion in the worldsheet genus
g
as:

\mathcal{A}_{n}=\sum_{g}\int DXDhe^{-S_{p}[X,h,\lambda]}\prod_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}=\sum_{g}e^{-\lambda\chi(g)}\int DXDhe^{-S_{p}[X,h]}\prod_{i=1}^{n}V_{i},

(3.7)

where the
V_{i}
, called vertex operators, encode the Lorentz degree of freedom of the external strings and depend on their energy state. After quantization, each term of the above expansion, in the closed string case, can be written as an integral over the
(3g-3+n)-
dimensional moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
of genus
g
Riemann surfaces with
n
punctures as

\mathcal{A}_{n}^{closed}\sim\int_{\mathcal{M}_{g,n}}d\lambda_{i}\int_{\Sigma^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}d^{2}z_{i}\prod_{i<j=1}^{n}|z_{ij}|^{2\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{i}k_{j}}|F(z_{ij},k)|^{2},

(3.8)

where
F
is a rational function and
z_{ij}\equiv z_{i}-z_{j}
.

A similar expression holds for a partial open amplitude, one finds:

\mathcal{A}_{n}^{open}(\sigma)\sim\int_{\mathcal{M}^{o}_{g,n}}d\lambda_{i}\int_{D(\sigma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,dx_{i}\prod_{i<j=1}^{n}|x_{ij}|^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{i}k_{j}}F(x_{ij},k),

(3.9)

with
D(\sigma)
the oriented punctured boundary of the open worldsheet and
M^{o}_{g,n}
the corresponding moduli space.

\mathcal{A}_{4}^{closed}=
+
+
+......

Figure 3.1:
Diagrammatic expansion of four closed string scattering.

3.1
Feynman Integrals

Despite manifest locality and causality, the configuration space quantities arising from (
3.4
), are very complicated convolutions of Bessel functions, whose evaluation is not known in the general case. In curved-space quantum field theories
[
48
]
, and more generally whenever translation invariance is broken, global Fourier transform is not available and configuration-space methods become indispensable; however, when this is not the case, it is convenient to work in momentum space, where the Feynman propagator is a rational function. We define the momentum space
n-
scattering amplitude
as the transform
n-
point Green function with all external states information cut out:
3
3

3

By ”amplitude” we will also refer to the full quantity, with external states included.

i\mathcal{A}\equiv\tilde{G}_{2}(p_{1})^{-1}\dots\tilde{G}_{2}(p_{n})^{-1}\tilde{G}_{n}^{c}(p_{1}\dots p_{n}).

(3.10)

So far, our discussion has been limited to scalar fields; however, realistic models require amplitudes to transform non-trivially under the symmetries of the theory, in order to capture the full set of degrees of freedom (i.e., spin, colour). Thus, the amplitude carries a multi-index in the appropriate representation (to be contracted with external states). Remarkably, all the extra structure can always be isolated by means of suitable projection techniques (tensor reduction) and all the topology information can be packaged into some scalar quantities
F_{i}
, called
form factors
:

\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}=\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\alpha}F_{i}.

(3.11)

Each form factor turns to be a linear combination of scalar multi-loop integral, called
Feynman integrals
(FIs).
These integrals are often ill-defined because of divergences arising for large (ultraviolet) or small (infrared) momentum. The latter are controlled by Kinoshita-Lee-Neuemberg theorem, ensuring they cancel when summed over all degenerate physical states while; on the other hand, in order to govern ultraviolet divergences, one identifies the analytic dependence of the divergence in term of auxiliary parameters (regularization) and then redefine the fields and the coupling constant (order by order) in such a way to absorb the poles (renormalization). The most standard regularization used in perturbative calculus is dimensional regularization, consisting in slightly shifting the space-time dimension away from the integer value
\lfloor D\rfloor
by setting
D=\lfloor D\rfloor+k\varepsilon
.

A generic
l-
loop Feynman integral in dimensional regularization, associate to a diagram with
N
external legs, has momentum space representation
4
4

4

Here:
\gamma_{E}
is Euler-Mascheroni constant,
k=-2
and
\mu
is mass scale parameter
:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}=e^{l\varepsilon\gamma_{E}}\left(\mu^{2}\right)^{\nu-lD/2}\int\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{d^{D}k}{i\pi^{D/2}}\frac{1}{D_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\dots D_{n}^{\nu_{n}}},

(3.12)

with
\nu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n=l(l+1)/2+lN
and where

D_{j}=q_{j}^{2}-m_{j}^{2},

(3.13)

with
q_{j}=q_{j}(k_{i},p_{k})
a linear function of loop momenta
k_{i}
and external momenta
p_{k}
.

Depending on the proprieties one wants manifestly to appear and the analysis one wants to carry on, the momentum space representation is often not the most suitable. Via some manipulations applied on (
3.12
) one can recover Feynman integrals expressed in different representations.
The detailed of the various derivations can be found in any text on the topic (see
[
187
]
for example), we just list here the essential information.
By using the following identity

\frac{1}{A^{\nu}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(\nu\right)}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d\alpha\,\alpha^{\nu-1}\,e^{-\alpha A},

(3.14)

called Schwinger trick, one menages to rewrite (
3.12
) in the so called
Schwinger representation
:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}=\frac{e^{l\varepsilon\gamma_{E}}}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\Gamma(\nu_{j})}\int\limits_{\alpha_{j}\geq 0}d^{n}\alpha\left(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{\nu_{j}-1}\right)\left[{\mathcal{U}}\left(\alpha\right)\right]^{-\frac{D}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{{\mathcal{F}}\left(\alpha\right)}{{\mathcal{U}}\left(\alpha\right)}\right),

(3.15)

with
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}
, called first and second
Symanzik polynomials
(or graph polynomials) degree
l
and
l+1
(respectively) homogeneous polynomials, given by

\mathcal{U}=\det(Q)\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\mathcal{F}=\mu^{-2}\det(Q)(J+\bm{\nu}^{T}Q^{-1}\bm{\nu}),

(3.16)

where
Q
and
J
are defined by

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}D_{j}=\bm{k}^{T}Q\bm{k}+2\bm{k}\bm{\nu}+J.

(3.17)

Expression (
3.15
) can be further manipulated: by inserting the identity

\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\delta\left(t-\sum\alpha_{j}\right)=1,

(3.18)

and making use of graph polynomials homogeneity, one can rewrite it as a projective integral in the homogeneous coordinates
x_{i}
of
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, obtaining the so called
Symanzik representation
, also named (Projective) Feynman parameter representation:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}=\frac{e^{l\varepsilon\gamma_{E}}\Gamma\left(\nu-\frac{lD}{2}\right)}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu_{j})}\int_{\Delta}\prod_{i}x_{i}^{\nu_{i}-1}\frac{\mathcal{U}(x_{i})^{\nu-(l+1)\frac{D}{2}}}{\mathcal{F}(x_{i})^{\nu-l\frac{D}{2}}}\mu,

(3.19)

where
\mu
is the measure of
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}

\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}x_{i}dx_{1}\wedge...\wedge\hat{dx_{i}}\wedge...\wedge dx_{n}

(3.20)

and the integration domain is the simplex

\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\supset\Delta=\{[x_{1}:x_{2}:...:x_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}|x_{i}\geq 0\}.

(3.21)

Consider now a diagram where the number of internal legs is related to the number of loops by

n=\frac{1}{2}l(l+1)+el,

(3.22)

with
e=\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{span}\{p_{1},\dots,p_{N}\})
.
5
5

5

Notice
e=n-1
for generic momenta and
D\geq N-1
.

In the momentum representation integral of such a diagram, the number of integration variables equals the number of irreducible scalar products
s_{j}
, namely all possible independent Lorentz invariants built up using loop and external momenta.
Furthermore, suppose any internal inverse propagator can be written as a linear combination of such invariants; that is, it exist an invertible matrix
C
such that
D_{i}=C_{ij}s_{j}+c_{i}
.

In this case, it is possible to use the denominators of (
3.12
) as new integration variables, setting
z_{i}=D_{i}
, and to express the integral in the so called
standard Baikov representation

[
18
,
19
,
20
]
(see also
[
106
]
for a review):

\mathcal{I}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}=\frac{e^{l\varepsilon\gamma_{E}}\left(\mu^{2}\right)^{\nu-\frac{lD}{2}}\left[\det G\left(p_{1},...,p_{e}\right)\right]^{\frac{-D+{e}+1}{2}}}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}\left(n-l\right)}\left(\det C\right)\prod\limits_{j=1}^{l}\Gamma\left(\frac{D-e+1-j}{2}\right)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}}d^{n}z\,\left[{\mathcal{B}}\left(\mathbf{z}\right)\right]^{\frac{D-l-{e}-1}{2}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}z_{s}^{-\nu_{i}};

(3.23)

where
G(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})=\det(p_{i}\cdot p_{j})
denotes the Gram determinant, the
Baikov polynomial

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z})
is given by

\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z})=G(k_{1},\dots,k_{l},p_{1},\dots,p_{e}),

(3.24)

and the integration domain is
\mathcal{C}\equiv\cap\mathcal{C}_{j}
with

\mathcal{C}_{j}\equiv\left\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\big|\frac{detG(k_{j},\dots,k_{l},p_{1},\dots,p_{e})}{detG(k_{j+1},\dots,k_{l},p_{1},\dots,p_{e})}\geq 0\right\}.

(3.25)

From now on, we will rewrite (
3.23
), in the compact form:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}\equiv K\int\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}\frac{d^{n}z}{z^{\nu_{i}}}.

(3.26)

A few comments are in order.
Despite the very strict conditions required for the Baikov change of variables (and its representation’s initial appearance as applicable only to a specialized subset of Feynman diagrams), it can be shown that any non-conforming graph is a subgraph of one that does satisfy the conditions, allowing it to inherit a Baikov representation from the larger graph.
While in most parametric representations of Feynman integrals, the number of integration variables equals the number of propagators, in the (standard) Baikov representation the number of variables grows quadratically with the number of loops, making it widely inefficient for high loop calculations. The attempt to reduce the variable count led to a modified version called loop-by-loop Baikov representation
[
88
,
87
]
.

A
Mathematica
package, named
BaikovPackage
, able to generate both standard and Loop-by-loop Baikov representations has been presented by Frellesvig in
[
90
]
.

Unitariry cuts.

The unitarity of the
S-
matrix,
S^{\dagger}S=1
, constrains the analytic structure of scattering amplitudes, tying together their imaginary part and the sums over intermediate on-shell states. The simplest and best known implication, concerning a scattering with one initial particle, is the
optical theorem
, forcing the imaginary part of a forward elastic scattering amplitude to equate the total total transition rate (total cross section) into all possible final states. In the complex energy plane this statement is equivalent to relating the discontinuity of the amplitude across its physical branch cut to the sum over on-shell intermediate contributions.
Cutkosky
[
67
]
proved a general diagrammatic prescription (
Cutkosky rules
) to compute the discontinuity of a perturbative Feynman amplitude: given a Feynman diagram, consider every possible cut that separates the diagram into two parts, called
unitarity cuts
, and places a subset of internal lines on shell. Each cut line with momentum
p
and mass
m
is replaced in the integrand by

\frac{1}{q^{2}-m^{2}+i0}\longrightarrow-2\pi i\delta_{+}(q^{2}-m^{2}),

(3.27)

enforcing the positive-energy on-shell condition. One then recovers the imaginary part by multiplying the left and right sub-amplitudes, integrating over the on-shell phase space of the cut lines, and summing over all allowed cuts.

This idea can been further extended beyond the Cutkosky sense, allowing for cuts not corresponding to physical discontinuities, including the one that do not separate the diagram. They are implemented as algebraic constrains at the integrand level by the imposition of on-shell conditions on a arbitrary subsets of propagators. These methods are the base of
generalized unitary

[
24
,
29
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
38
,
39
,
6
]
(see
[
28
]
for a review).
A special role is played by the so called
maximal cut

[
34
]
, where the largest possible number (compatibly with the topology) of internal propagators
are imposed on-shell. Maximal cuts compute the leading singularities of the integrand, ie. the residues at the global poles of the loop integrand in complexified momentum space. These residues are highly constrained objects that serve as robust building blocks for systematically reconstructing the integrand. As we will see, FIs turn be solutions of differential equations in the kinematic variables; the corresponding maximal cut has the propriety to solve the associated homogenized differential equation.

Unitarity cuts are very naturally implemented in Baikov representation: a
k-cut
on a Feynman integral expressed as in (
3.23
) is obtained by taking the multiple residue at the simultaneous pole
z_{i}=\dots=z_{i+k}=0
:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}|_{k-cut}\sim\int\limits_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}d^{n-k}z\,\oint d^{k}z\left[{\mathcal{B}}\left(\mathbf{z}\right)\right]^{\frac{D-l-{e}-1}{2}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}z_{s}^{-\nu_{i}}.

(3.28)

In
[
182
]
, Vanhove shows, through toric-geometric arguments, that the maximal cut also admits a nice Symanzik parametrization obtained by simply replacing the simplex
\Delta
by the
l-
torus
T^{l}=\{z_{i}\in\mathbb{P}^{n+1}|z_{i}=1\}
:

I=\int_{T^{l}}\prod_{i}x_{i}^{\nu_{i}-1}\frac{\mathcal{U}(x_{i})^{\nu-(l+1)\frac{D}{2}}}{\mathcal{F}(x_{i})^{\nu-l\frac{D}{2}}}\mu\,.

(3.29)

3.2
Master Integrals and periods

High order perturbative integrals are extremely tough to compute analytically and their numerical evaluation requires huge computational effort. Moreover, the number of integrals contributing to the amplitude at a given order, grows at least exponentially with the latter. These two facts make direct high order corrections computation impossible.
The crucial simplification allowing to bypass this obstacle, firstly observed
[
63
]
, is to realise that the Feynman integrals in a given family are not all independent, but satisfy linear relations coming from Stokes’ theorem, known as integration by parts identities, IBP’s. Implemented algorithmically by Laporta in
[
127
]
, IBP reduction turns the problem of handling an enormous set of integrals into a finite linear-algebra task: one generates many IBP relations, orders integrals by complexity and eliminates systematically to express every integral in the family as a linear combination of a small, finite set of irreducible objects, called
master integrals
. Once the masters are identified, their dependence on kinematics can be obtained by differential equations or other means and the entire family is reconstructed from their solutions. More conceptually, it was observed by Mastrolia and Mizera in
[
137
]
, and later refined in
[
84
,
86
]
, that Feynman integrals naturally span a finite-dimensional vector space which can be understood in cohomological terms: integrals are pairings of (twisted) cycles and cocycles, IBP relations correspond to exact forms or boundaries, and intersection theory provides a nondegenerate pairing that lets one project any integrand onto a chosen basis of masters. This geometric viewpoint not only explains why the number of masters is finite but also suggests computational alternatives to brute force elimination by computing intersection numbers and exploiting algebraic geometry one can often obtain reduction coefficients more efficiently and gain structural insight into when and why certain masters evaluate to polylogarithmic, elliptic, or more general period functions.

Let us see how this ideas comes in the most naturally way.

Consider a set of integrals

I_{ij}=\int_{c_{i}}\omega_{j}(\mathbf{z}),

(3.30)

where
\omega_{j}
, with
j\in\{1,\dots,N_{\omega}\}
, are multi-variable
n-
forms depending on
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
and
c_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,\dots,N_{c}\}
, are
n-
dimensional integration contours.

If we find a equivalence relation
\sim
among integrands such that

\omega_{j}\sim\omega_{k}\quad\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\quad I_{ij}=I_{ik},

(3.31)

we identify
N_{1}\leq N_{\omega}
equivalence classes
\braket{\omega}_{J}
, reducing the number of independent integrals from
N_{\omega}N_{c}
to
N_{1}N_{c}
.

There is still one source of interdependence: due to the linearity of integration, the equivalence classes we have defined are actually subjected to the further relation:

\braket{\omega}_{J}\sim\braket{\omega}_{K}+\braket{\omega}_{L}\quad\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\quad I_{iJ}=I_{iK}+I_{iL},

(3.32)

reducing the number of independent equivalence classes to
\nu\leq N_{1}
.
Let us pick up one representative element
\ket{\phi_{\beta}}
per equivalence class.

We can apply the very same reasoning to contours, identifying
\tilde{\nu}
equivalence classes
[\gamma_{\tilde{\alpha}}|
.

In order for integration not to be degenerate there must be
\tilde{\nu}=\nu
.

Defining now the vector spaces
\mathbb{V}=\mathrm{span}\{\ket{\phi_{\beta}}\}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\vee}=\mathrm{span}\{[\gamma_{\beta}|\}
, we can express any integrand and any contour appearing in (
3.30
) as a linear combinations of the respective basis elements as

[c_{i}|=a_{i}^{\,\alpha}[\gamma_{\alpha}|\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\ket{\omega}_{j}=b_{j}^{\,\beta}\ket{\phi_{\beta}}.

(3.33)

Integration naturally induces a scalar product among these vector spaces, that actually turn to be dual.

We finally end up with a subset of independent
\nu^{2}
integrals

I_{\alpha\beta}=[\gamma_{\alpha}\ket{\phi_{\beta}}

(3.34)

called (a basis of)
Master integrals
, completely determining a whole family of integrals, even larger then the starting one, by

I_{ij}=a_{i}^{\,\alpha}b_{j}^{\,\beta}I_{\alpha\beta}.

(3.35)

These vector spaces are not just ad-hoc linear reductions: they exactly are the (co)homology
n
-th groups of (co)chain complex, over some space
\mathcal{M}
, with (co)boundary operators the one inducing the equivalence relations above.

The master integral matrix is then nothing else that the period matrix we saw in section
2.2.3
.

We call
geometry
associated to a set of integrals the space whose periods provide a master integral basis for it.

Integrals
I_{ij}(\bm{\lambda})
appearing in perturbative calculations actually depends on extra parameters
\bm{\lambda}
, i.e. the kinematic variables and regularization parameter,
thus also the basis of master integrals would depend on them, so following the above reasoning and section
2.2.3
, that would be a family of complex manifold
M_{\bm{\lambda}}
whose periods are such that:

\Pi_{\alpha\beta}(\bm{\lambda})=I_{\alpha\beta}(\bm{\lambda}).

(3.36)

Summarizing, the challenges one has to face for successfully manage to apply intersection theory to handle this families of integrals are:

•

Identify which (co)homology groups capture the equivalence relations among the considered integrals;

•

Compute such groups and in particular determining their dimension, corresponding to the number of MIs of the family;

•

Evaluate the coefficients appearing in the decomposition (
3.33
) by defining suitable
intersection pairings
;

•

Identify the underlying geometry, that is finding a family
M_{\bm{\Lambda}}
of algebraic varieties and its moduli space whose periods computes the MIs.

•

Determine the Picard-Fuchs equation governing the periods of
M_{\bm{\Lambda}}
.

3.2.1
The sought (co)homology

The first step is to recognize the appropriate (co)homology groups capturing the structure of the integral family we are considering.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth
n-
dimensional manifold and
\mathcal{L}
a local system on
\mathcal{M}
with connection
1-
form
\omega
. A flat sections
u
of
\mathcal{L}
, solves the differential equation
\nabla_{\omega}u\equiv(d+\omega)u(\mathbf{z})=0
, in a coordinate system
z_{i}
, and can thus be written as

u=c\,e^{\lambda},

(3.37)

with
d\lambda=\omega
.

Global sections
\phi_{R}
of the sheaf
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L})
of
k-
forms taking value in
\mathcal{L}
, because of the isomorphism
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L})\cong\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{L}
, can be expressed as

\phi_{R}(\mathbf{z})=u(\mathbf{z})\phi(\mathbf{z}),

(3.38)

for
\phi(\mathbf{z})
a global section of
\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{C})
. Let us call
\phi_{R}
a (
k-
)
right form
.

In order for a right form to be closed

d(\phi_{R})=du\phi+ud\phi=-\omega u\phi+ud\phi=u\nabla_{-\omega}\phi,

(3.39)

\phi
must be closed with respect to the covariant derivative
\nabla_{-\omega}\equiv d-\omega
, that is it must belong to the
k-
th cohomology group of the complex

\Omega^{\bullet}_{-\omega}:\dots\,\rightarrow\Omega^{k-1}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\nabla_{-\omega}}\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\nabla_{-\omega}}\dots

(3.40)

Denote by
H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{-\omega})\equiv H^{k}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{-\omega})\otimes\mathcal{L}
the group of closed right
k-
forms
\ket{\phi_{R}}
, called
twisted cocycles
.

The homology groups dual to this are given by
H_{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})\equiv H_{k}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{-\omega})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vee}
, where
H_{k}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{-\omega})^{\vee}
is the cohomology of the dual complex and
\mathcal{L}^{\vee}
is the local system with connection
\omega
. Let us call the elements of this group
twisted cycles
and denote them by
[C_{L}|
.

There is then a non degenerating pairing given by:

[C_{L}\ket{\phi_{R}}=\int_{C}\phi_{R}.

(3.41)

Now, Feynman Integrals in Baikov representation (
3.26
)
6
6

6

After complexification.
, as well as string amplitudes (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), have the form

I=\int_{C}\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}(\mathbf{z})\phi(\mathbf{z}),

(3.42)

where
\phi(\mathbf{z})
is a rational
n-
form,

\phi(\mathbf{z})=\frac{dz_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dz_{n}}{z_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\dots z_{n}^{\nu_{n}}}

(3.43)

and
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}
is a multivalued function, with
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{z})
a polynomial and
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{Z}
.

Thus they can be recognized as a twisted pairing (
3.41
) up to the identification of the suitable space
\mathcal{M}
and local system
\mathcal{L}
.

We need
\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}
to be a flat section of
\mathcal{L}
, thus solving
\nabla_{\omega}\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}=0
for
\omega
, and hence we get

\omega=dlog\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}=\gamma\left(\frac{\partial_{i}\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}\right)dz_{i}\,.

(3.44)

Moreover, we need
\omega
to be globally defined on
\mathcal{M}
. Thus, if
\mathcal{V}[\mathcal{B}]\equiv\{\mathcal{B}=0\}
is smooth, we have
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}\setminus\{\mathcal{B}=0\}
.
We stress here that a first problem may arise if
\mathcal{V}[\mathcal{B}]
is singular.

Moreover, being
\mathcal{V}[\mathcal{B}]
non compact, we need to control the behavior at infinity; the conclusion proved by Pham
[
157
]
, is that the right cohomology for describing an integral of the form (
3.42
), up to the assumptions discussed above, is the cohomology group
H_{n}(\mathcal{M},D_{\infty})
relative to a neighbordhood of infinity
D_{\infty}
.

Number of MIs.

In practical calculations, one generally does not need to algebraically determine the (co)homology groups, but rather to just compute their dimension
\nu
, corresponding to the number of MIs. A lack of the algorithms based on IBPs relations is indeed the impossibility to know
\nu
a priori, thus one has to keep trying generating independent elements until the procedure reach the closure. However, if the number of MIs is known, the generation of a basis is a relative easy task.

Under the assumptions given in
[
8
]
(see also
[
9
,
10
,
107
]
) one can prove the following vanishing theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

(Vanishing theorem)

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{P}
be the
n-
dimensional complex manifold complementary to the projective variety
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}[P]
defined by
m
polynomials
P_{j}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and
\nabla_{\omega}
the covariant derivative, with connection
\omega
, of a rank-1 local system
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathcal{M}
.

The only possibly non vanishing twisted cohomology group is the one in middle degree:

H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})=0\quad\quad\mbox{for}\quad k\neq n.

(3.45)

By Euler-Poincaré formula
7
7

7

\chi(X)=\sum(-1)^{k}H_{k}(X,\mathbb{C})
.
we then have

\dim H^{n}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})=(-1)^{n}\chi(\mathcal{M}).

(3.46)

and thus:

\nu=(-1)^{n}(n+1-\chi(\mathcal{P})).

(3.47)

The computation of the Euler characteristic for the projective variety
\mathcal{P}
is actually non-trivial.

However, we can compute
\chi(\mathcal{M})
by means of Morse theory
[
144
]
(see
[
136
]
for a simple introduction).
Let
f
be a Morse function for
\mathcal{M}
, and
k_{p}
the number of its critical points with Morse index
p
. Because of the vanishing theorem there are no critical points with index
k\neq n
and thus all critical points must have the same, i.e. the highest, Morse index;
k_{n}
is therefore the total number of critical points of
f
, and it must equal the
n-
th Betti number
b_{n}
:

\nu=b_{n}=k_{n}.

(3.48)

In
[
128
]
, Lee and Pomeransky show that
\log{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}}
is a Morse function for
\mathcal{M}
, thus the number of its critical points, the solutions
\omega=0
, gives the Euler characteristic of
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, we have reduced the problem of determining the number of master integrals to the resolution of a system of equations,
provided the vanishing theorem
3.45
holds.
We stress indeed, the latter is necessary for the Euler characteristic of
\mathcal{M}
to coincide, up to a possible sign, with
\nu
; if some other cohomology groups appear, we would eventually need to know their dimensions for evaluating
\nu
via Euler-Poincaré formula.

Among the assumptions of the above theorem, it is required that the monodromy of
\mathcal{L}
around each irreducible component of
\mathcal{P}
and their intersections must
not
be trivial, i.e. equal to one. This is a necessary condition for killing cohomology group outside the middle one.

3.2.2
Intersection numbers

Once a basis of Master integrals is provided, we need to be able to decompose any other integral of the family in terms of the basis, that is to compute the coefficients appearing in the (
3.33
).
We need to identify dual vector spaces
\mathbb{W}^{\vee}=\mathrm{span}\{\bra{\phi^{\beta}}\}
and
\mathbb{W}=\mathrm{span}\{|\gamma_{\alpha}]\}
, and scalar products

\braket{\cdot|\cdot}:\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{W}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad[\cdot|\cdot]:\mathbb{V}^{\vee}\times\mathbb{W}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},

(3.49)

such that, provided the basis are orthonormally chosen

[\gamma_{\alpha}|\gamma^{\beta}]=\braket{\phi^{\beta}|\phi_{\alpha}}=\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha},

(3.50)

we can write:

a_{i}^{\alpha}=[c_{i}|\gamma^{\alpha}]\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad b_{j}^{\beta}=\braket{\phi^{\beta}|\omega_{j}}.

(3.51)

For a general reasonable topological space
\mathcal{M}
, we have seen in section
2.1.6
that such dual spaces are provided by Verdier duality (
2.80
); however, no general construction of such dual objects is known, and its concrete and explicit realization for practical purposes is an open problem.
However, if we restrict to local systems on locally compact oriented manifolds, the twisted version of Poincaré duality (
2.89
) gives us

\begin{split}&H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L})\times H^{k}_{BM}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L}^{\vee})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\\
&H_{k}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L})\times H_{k}^{lf}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{L}^{\vee})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\end{split}

(3.52)

revealing the sought dual spaces are the one of compactly supported twisted cocycles and locally finite twisted cycles.
The computation of homological intersection numbers is performed via diagrammatical methods for which we remand to
[
116
,
115
,
146
,
149
,
151
]
(see also
[
136
]
), while below we will report a brief review of cohomological intersection numbers evaluation methods.

Cohomological intersection numbers

Let
\mathcal{M}
be
n-
dimensional,
\phi_{R}\in H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{-\omega})
and
\phi^{c}_{L}\in H^{k}_{BM}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})
, with
n=2k
. A realization for the pairing

H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})\times H^{k}_{BM}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{-\omega})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},

(3.53)

is simply provided by the integration

\braket{\phi^{c}_{L}|\phi_{L}}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\phi^{c}_{L}\wedge\psi_{R},

(3.54)

induced by the cup product.

Now, because
H^{k}_{BM}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})
, we can extend the pairing to all twisted cocycles

\braket{\phi_{R}|\phi_{L}}=\braket{\iota(\phi_{R})|\phi_{L}},

(3.55)

where
\iota:H^{k}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})\rightarrow H^{k}_{BM}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})
, is called
regularization map
. Roughly speaking, any cohomology class has a representative with compact support; the formula (
3.54
) provides a way to compute the intersection with that form, but due the to invariance of the integral map inside the same class, the intersection must be the same for any chosen representative.
The problem then reduces to find a realization of the regularization map.

First consider the case of a holomorphic one form.

Let
U_{j}\subset V_{j}\subset\mathcal{M}
be two sets of neighborhoods of
z_{j}\in\mathcal{M}
, and let
h_{j}
a partition of unity

h_{j}(z)=\begin{cases}&1\quad\quad z\in U_{j},\\
&0\quad\quad z\notin V_{j}.\end{cases}

(3.56)

Let
\phi_{L}(z)\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{M},\nabla_{\omega})
. By Poincaré lemma, it exists
\psi_{j}(z)\in\Omega^{0}(V_{j},\nabla_{\omega})
such that
\nabla_{\omega}\psi_{j}(z)=\phi_{L}(z)
, for
z\in V_{j}
. Introduce the one form

\phi^{\prime}_{L}\equiv\phi_{L}-\nabla_{\omega}(h_{j}\psi_{j})=\begin{cases}&0\quad\mbox{if}\,z\in U_{j}\\
&\phi_{L}\quad\mbox{if}\,z\notin V_{j},\end{cases}

(3.57)

with support

\mathrm{supp}[\phi^{\prime}_{L}]\subset\left(\bigcup_{j}U_{j}\right)^{c},

(3.58)

where the superscript
c
here stands for the complementary set in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Because
U_{j}
are open, their complementary is closed, and thus
\phi^{\prime}
has compact support. Therefore, we get the regularization map:

\iota(\phi_{L})=\phi_{L}-\nabla_{\omega}(h_{j}\psi_{j}).

(3.59)

Plugging this result into (
3.54
), after some algebra, and using residue theorem, one obtains

\langle\phi_{L}|\phi_{R}\rangle=\sum_{j}\mathrm{Res}_{z=z_{j}}(\psi_{j}\phi_{R}).

(3.60)

The computation of the intersection number for one forms then reduces to solve a differential equation around each pole and to compute residues at each of those poles.

Expression (
3.60
) assumes a further simplified form whenever
\phi_{L}
or
\phi_{R}
have only simple poles:

\langle\phi_{L}|\phi_{R}\rangle=\sum_{j}\frac{\mathrm{Res}_{z=z_{j}}\psi_{j}\mathrm{Res}_{z=z_{j}}\phi_{R}}{\alpha_{j}},

(3.61)

where
\alpha_{j}
are the residues of
\omega
at
z_{j}
.

In
[
64
]
, Cho and Matsumoto proved that (
3.61
) can be generalized to the case of logarithmic
n-
form. Moreover, in
[
150
]
Mizera showed it can be expressed in terms of the zeroes
z_{ij}^{*}
of
\omega
, instead of its poles:

\langle\phi_{L}|\phi_{R}\rangle=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}\delta[\omega_{i}]\phi_{L}\phi_{R}=\sum_{z^{*}_{ij}}|\partial_{i}\omega_{j}|^{-1}\phi_{L}\phi_{R}\Big|_{z_{i}=z^{*}_{ij}},

(3.62)

which actually turns to be a big computational advantage, due to the greater ease to practically identify the zeros rather than the poles.

Deligne’s theorem

H^{k}(U,\mathbb{C})\cong H^{k}(X,\Omega^{\bullet}(\log D))

(3.63)

guarantees every cohomology class has a logarithmic form representative. Thus (
3.62
) should suffice to evaluate any intersection number. However, except in the particular case of a hyperplane arrangement and the trivial case one variable case, there is no known realization of such map, in the sense of a general computationally implementable prescription to find a logarithmic equivalent to a general rational
n-
form.
To overcome this, a fibration-based method was introduced in
[
154
,
84
,
85
]
and recently refined in

[
54
,
55
,
82
,
41
]
, consisting in an iterative procedure of degree reduction, through which
n-
forms intersection numbers are reduced to
1-
forms intersections.

3.2.3
Canonical differential equation

Consider the period vector
\Pi_{i}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon)
, satisfying the system of Picard-Fuchs equations

\partial^{\nu}\Pi_{i}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon)=\Omega_{ij}^{\nu}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon)\Pi_{j}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon),

(3.64)

with
\partial^{\nu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{\nu}}
.
Because Gauss-Manin connection is flat (covariant derivatives commutes), the connection 1-forms
\Omega^{\nu}
obey the integrability condition

\partial^{\mu}\Omega^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}\Omega^{\mu}+[\Omega^{\nu},\Omega^{\mu}].

(3.65)

Under a (holomorphic) change of basis
\widetilde{\Pi}=\Lambda\Pi
, the connection matrices transform by the usual guage rule

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mu}=\Lambda\Omega^{\mu}\Lambda-\Lambda^{-1}\partial^{\mu}\Lambda.

(3.66)

In
[
110
]
, Henn prososed that there exists a gauge
\Lambda
such that the dependence on the dimensional regulator factors out:

\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mu}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\Omega^{\mu}(\bm{\lambda}),

(3.67)

requiring the condition
8
8

8

One can easily see non commutativity of connection matrices is a obstraction to higher order in
\varepsilon
to identically vanish.

[\widetilde{\Omega}^{\mu},\widetilde{\Omega}^{\nu}]=0.

(3.68)

In this basis, the differential equation (
3.64
) reduces to the so called
\varepsilon-
factorized form

\partial^{\nu}\widetilde{\Pi}_{i}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\widetilde{\Omega}_{ij}^{\nu}(\bm{\lambda})\widetilde{\Pi}_{j}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon),

(3.69)

whose solution can be written in terms of path-ordered exponential

\widetilde{\Pi}(\bm{\lambda},\varepsilon)=\left(\mathcal{P}e^{\varepsilon\int_{C}d\widetilde{\Omega}}\right)\Pi(\bm{\lambda},0).

(3.70)

If this trivializing gauge transformation is found, then the task of solving
\varepsilon-
expanded master integrals is essentially solved.

The global existence of a such gauge transformation implies that the structure group of the cohomology bundle admits a reduction to an abelian subgroup, that is, that monodromy matrices commute. However, being interested in study MIs for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we just need this transformation to exist locally, in which case the above strong condition, although clearly sufficient, it is not necessary any more
[
131
,
180
,
185
,
21
]
:

Provided
\varepsilon=0
is a regular singular point for (
3.64
) and (
3.68
) holds, local
\varepsilon-
factorized form exist.

If
\Omega(\lambda,\varepsilon)
has at most simple poles in
\varepsilon
, there exist local holomoprhic gauge transformation putting (
3.64
) in the
Levelt form
:

\frac{d\Pi}{d\bm{\Lambda}}=\frac{R}{\varepsilon}\Pi,

(3.71)

with
R
in Jordan normal form.

On the other hand, if
\varepsilon=0
is an irregular point, Levelt-Turrittin theorem states that, possibily after a finite ramified cover
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{p}
(
p\in\mathbb{Z}
), and a formal
9
9

9

Not necessary converging Laurent power series.
